The kelch13 corroboration is weaker than previously claimed. Pf8 also publishes a kelch13 resistance status, and the previous version leaned on it as independent support for the artemisinin negative. That support must be stated far more weakly. Pf8 assigns the status over the whole 349–726 propeller domain and classifies any missing call in that range as undetermined — "Any missing call in amino acids 349-726 → Missing → Undetermined" — so only 36 isolates carry a complete status. Thirty-six complete statuses are weak corroboration for per-codon calls on roughly 1,860 isolates, and the two are not equivalent evidence. The per-sample concordance above, on 25 markers and 47,012 calls, exercises the same CDS mapping, strand handling and codon arithmetic used throughout the panel, so it is reassuring about the method; but it contains no pfk13 codon at all, so it is not evidence about the artemisinin negative, for which these 36 statuses are the only external corroboration that exists. The kelch13 status is mentioned for completeness and nothing rests on it.

What the external cross-check bought, twice. The first version of this validation caught Stage 10's ANN_Allele indexing bug, which reported pfmdr1 Asn86Tyr as "Asn86Asp" and Tyr184Phe as "Tyr184Ser" — correct frequencies, wrong variant names — and which no internal consistency check could have found, because nothing was out of range or implausible. The second version, moving from marginal agreement to call-by-call agreement, is what made the pfcrt 75 problem legible as a method failure rather than a tolerance failure. Both times the error that escaped internal checking was an error of external correctness: whether the marker list matched an authoritative source, whether the annotation direction matched the reference genome, whether a co-occurrence claim had co-occurrence data behind it. The verification architecture in this project was built to catch stale numbers, and it did. It was not built to ask whether the list was the right list.

3.11 False-discovery accounting in the XtX scan

The problem. A quantile threshold is not a significance test. Calling a SNP an outlier when its XtX exceeds the 0.999 quantile of the pseudo-observed null guarantees that 0.1 per cent of strictly neutral SNPs will be called, by construction, however neutral the genome is. The original Stage 8 reported a threshold and a count of SNPs above it, with no statement of how many were expected under the null — so the outlier list had no error rate attached, and the reader had no way to judge how much of it to believe.

What the step now reports. step08c_calibrate_xtx.py was extended to report four things alongside the threshold.

Expected false positives at the threshold. The quantile and the panel size give this immediately: at the 0.999 quantile over 3,916 SNPs, 3.9 are expected above threshold under the null.

An expected-null-exceedance ratio at the threshold — expected false positives under the assumed null divided by the number of SNPs actually called. It is an expectation under that null, not a measured false-discovery fraction. With 19 SNPs above a threshold of XtX 10.840 and 3.9 expected, that is 20.6 per cent.

Per-SNP p-values against the pseudo-observed null. Each observed XtX is ranked within the sorted pseudo-observed distribution and assigned p = (n_pod − rank + 1) / (n_pod + 1), which is a Monte Carlo one-sided tail probability under that fitted null and is bounded below by 1/(n_pod + 1) rather than being reported as zero.

Benjamini–Hochberg q-values, computed over all 3,916 SNPs by the standard step-up procedure with monotonicity enforced (step08c_calibrate_xtx.py:114, with the write at :139). The q-values are the values in Table S12, and logs/stage8.log:115-119 prints the counts at q ≤ 0.01, 0.05, 0.1 and 0.2. The previous revision had to report the per-SNP artefact as unusable, because results/baypass/xtx_all_snps.csv on the server was 0 bytes — no header, no rows, no q-value column. That file has since been found truncated and regenerated from the intact BayPass outputs, so a per-SNP file is on disk again; Section 3.13.1 records the truncation, the regeneration and the consistency checks the regenerated file passed. The citable sources for the final q-values remain results/baypass/xtx_calibration.json and the printed counts in the Stage 8 log, and those are what this manuscript cites; the restored per-SNP file is now consistent with them rather than a substitute for them.

[Calculated] The restored per-SNP q-values reproduce the calibration counts exactly. The 19 outliers' q-values in results/outlier_quality_check.csv give 8 SNPs at q ≤ 0.01, 10 at q ≤ 0.05, 13 at q ≤ 0.10 and 17 at q ≤ 0.20, which are the four counts in results/baypass/xtx_calibration.json and in Table S12. That is an independent arithmetic check on the regeneration, and it passes.

Table S12. The XtX outlier set under the quantile rule and under Benjamini–Hochberg control. Of the 19 SNPs above the quantile threshold, 13 also satisfy q ≤ 0.10 and 6 do not. Values from results/baypass/xtx_calibration.json, and reproduced from the restored per-SNP file (Section 3.13.1).

[Interpreted] How the two are reported. Both, with their roles distinguished. The quantile list is a ranking: it orders SNPs by evidence and is the right object for asking which genes to look at. The q-value subset is the test: it is the set that survives multiplicity control at a stated error rate. Sections 6.8 and 7 therefore report 13 SNPs as the finding and 19 as the ranking, rather than choosing one number and suppressing the other. The step prints the comparison explicitly so that the two cannot drift apart in later write-ups. Neither number is a gene count: 17 of the 19 fall in annotated genes, across 13 genes, and two are intergenic (Section 3.9). A third qualification now applies to both lists: two of the 19 are downgraded to lower confidence on the quality grounds of Section 3.11.1, and one of those two is fifth in the ranking.

Caveat. Neither procedure addresses non-independence. No LD pruning or clumping is applied before counting, so two outlier SNPs in strong linkage within one gene enter the count as two tests and are reported per gene as two SNPs. Benjamini–Hochberg is robust to positive dependence in the sense of controlling the FDR, but the per-gene SNP counts in Section 6.8 should not be read as independent lines of evidence. The pfaat1 pair, two sites 883 bp apart — Pf3D7_06_v3:1,214,350 and 1,215,233, both PF3D7_0629500 — is reported as one signal there. It is not, however, the clearest instance, and that superlative is withdrawn: it was carried over from the superseded 15-outlier panel, on which pfaat1 did top the r² ranking. Recomputed on interim/ghana_core_snps.npz (3,916 SNPs, 1,900 isolates, haploid allele matrix, pairwise-complete) over every same-chromosome outlier pair within 200 kb, r² is 0.828 for the PF3D7_0221200 pair at Pf3D7_02_v3:855,467 and 855,665 (198 bp), 0.674 for the ACS8/PF3D7_0215300 pair at 630,290 and 630,420 (130 bp), then 0.602 for pfaat1, with the PF3D7_0831400 pair at 25 bp and r² = 0.398. The pfaat1 pair is third by r² and the widest-spaced of the four same-gene pairs; where a strongest instance is wanted, it is the PF3D7_0221200 pair, and the measure is stated with it.

3.11.1 Quality audit of the outlier set

What and why. The outlier list is the one result in this manuscript that names individual sites, so it is the one most exposed to a mapping or calling artefact masquerading as differentiation — the specific worry being that several of the hits lie in exported-protein genes whose families are repetitive. That worry had been raised but never tested. This check tests it by reading what the release itself says about each site's call quality, which requires no new inference and no new model.

Which annotations, and against what. For each of the 19 XtX outliers the Pf8 per-variant annotations were pulled from the Zarr and compared against the other 3,897 SNPs of the same scanned panel — the 3,916 that entered Stage 8, less the 19. The comparator is deliberate: the scanned panel has already passed FILTER_PASS, biallelic and is_snp, the depth floor, variant missingness and MAF ≥ 0.05, so comparing the outliers to it asks whether they are unusual among the sites the scan could have called, whereas comparing them to all Pf8 sites would confound the scan's own ascertainment with the question. The fields read are the mapping and strand-bias annotations MQ, FS and SOR; the variant-recalibration score VQSLOD and the FILTER_Low_VQSLOD, NEGATIVE_TRAIN_SITE and POSITIVE_TRAIN_SITE flags; the quality-by-depth statistics QD, QUAL and DP; the rank-sum tests BaseQRankSum, MQRankSum and ReadPosRankSum; the allele number AN; the heterozygosity statistics ExcessHet and InbreedingCoeff; the culprit field, which names the annotation most responsible for a site's VQSR score; and RegionType. Each outlier also carries a percentile within the panel for the main metrics, and three local-context measures computed from the panel itself: the number of scanned SNPs within 1 kb and 10 kb, the local mean MQ in those windows, and the number of other outliers within 10 kb. Every outlier is then assigned a flag level — clean, watch or flag — with a verdict string naming the annotation responsible. Per-SNP table in results/outlier_quality_check.csv.

[Observed] Of the 19, 15 are clean, 2 are watch and 2 are flag. All 19 are RegionType Core, none has MQ below 50 (the minimum is 59.56), and none shows strand bias or read-position bias. Every significant difference against the rest of the panel runs in the outliers' favour: higher VQSLOD (p = 0.008), QD (p = 0.016) and QUAL (p = 0.007). The culprit field reads MQRankSum for all 19, but also for 99.6 per cent of the rest of the panel, so it carries no information here and is reported only to say so.

[Observed] The exported-protein concern is not supported. The eight SNPs in PTP7, PIESP2, GEXP22, REX2, PF3D7_0831400 and PF3D7_0221200 are the cleanest subset in the panel rather than the most suspect: FS = 0.00 for all eight, MQ median 60.00, and VQSLOD significantly higher than the rest. The families are repetitive; these particular sites are in uniquely mappable sequence, and the two statements are compatible. The concern was worth raising and the answer is that it does not bite here.

[Observed] Two SNPs are downgraded to lower confidence, on different grounds. Pf3D7_11_v3:1054078 (SNRPF, XtX 13.98, q = 0.031) and Pf3D7_11_v3:1054447 (intergenic, XtX 20.79, q = 0.005, fifth-ranked in the whole scan) lie 369 bp apart and are the two lowest-InbreedingCoeff sites in the panel (0.147 at the 0.08 percentile and 0.193 at the 0.13 percentile); both also sit in the lowest percentile of QD (12.91 and 11.00, both at the 0.3 percentile), and 1054447 has VQSLOD 5.58, at the 2.4 percentile. 1054078 carries ExcessHet = 69.59, the only nonzero value among all 3,916 SNPs and roughly 400 times the genome-wide maximum. Both are reported as lower confidence from here on.

[Hypothesized] In a near-haploid organism an excess of heterozygous calls at one site, with clean MQ (59.99) and FS (0.00), points to collapsed paralogy or copy-number variation rather than to mismapping: reads from two similar copies piling onto one locus produce apparent heterozygotes without degrading mapping quality or introducing strand bias. Two adjacent sites sharing the signature is consistent with one such region rather than two independent accidents.

[Unknown] Whether the chromosome-11 pair sits in a collapsed duplication. The annotations are consistent with it and do not establish it.

[Proposed] Settle the mechanism with a read-level check: per-sample depth at these two positions as a ratio to each sample's genome-median depth. A collapsed duplication gives roughly twice the median across most samples; a calling artefact at a single-copy locus does not. The data are in the local store, the check needs no new retrieval, and it would either retire the fifth-ranked outlier or restore it to full confidence. Until it is done, the downgrade stands and Section 6.8 reports both SNPs with it.

What this check can and cannot do. It compares annotations, not reads. A site can carry excellent MQ, VQSLOD, FS and SOR and still be wrong, because every one of those statistics is computed from the same alignment; if reads are mismapped consistently, the annotations will look clean. So "no evidence of an artefact" is the finding, and "no artefact" is not. The check is also not a test of selection: that the 19 are well-called says nothing about why their XtX is high. Its value is narrow and real — it removes a specific alternative explanation for eight of the hits, and it finds a specific problem at two that the XtX ranking alone would never have surfaced.

3.12 Stage 11 — is zone separable from sequencing study?

What and why. The manuscript's design-limitations section listed three confounders of zone: collinearity with administrative region, coordinate resolution at admin level 1, and differing collection years. A fourth was missing. Pf8 isolates arrive through named partner studies, and in Ghana those studies are close to nested within zones. Where no study contributes isolates to both zones of a comparison, that comparison is simultaneously a between-batch comparison, and no analysis of these data can separate the two. This stage quantifies that rather than asserting it. It is read-only and changes no pipeline output.

Method. Four diagnostics are computed from the sample manifest and the Stage 3b quality metrics, over the 4,905 release-QC-passing isolates that carry both a study and a zone label — not over the 1,900-isolate analysis set. The denominator is deliberate: batch structure is a property of how the isolates were collected and sequenced, so it is measured before clonal pruning and the missingness cuts rather than after them.

A study-by-zone cross-tabulation, and for each of the six zone pairs the number of sequencing studies contributing isolates to both.

Whether studies differ in within-host fixation index within a zone — zone held constant, so that study varies together with collection year, finer geography, recruitment and host biology, which tests for study-associated heterogeneity rather than isolating a technical batch cause.

The same test for call rate within a zone.

For the zone pairs that do share a study, whether the within-study difference agrees in sign with the overall between-zone difference. This is the one check that can partially exonerate a gradient, and it is reported whichever way it comes out.

[Observed] Eleven studies contribute the 4,905 isolates. 4 of the 6 zone pairs have no sequencing study in common, including Coastal savanna against Guinea savanna — the pair that carries the maximum pairwise FST and the polyclonality contrast. Coastal savanna against Deciduous forest shares 2 studies and Guinea savanna against Transitional zone shares 1; the remaining four share none. Guinea savanna draws on six studies, Deciduous forest on five, Coastal savanna on two, and the Transitional zone on one (1083-PF-GH-CONWAY, which supplies all 50 of its isolates at this stage). Within-zone heterogeneity between studies is severe where it can be tested: call rate differs between studies within Deciduous forest at p = 7.7 × 10⁻⁸⁸ and within Guinea savanna at p = 2.8 × 10⁻¹⁹⁷ (results/batch_within_zone_tests.csv).

Five studies, four tested. The within-zone tests cover only studies meeting the minimum-n threshold, so results/batch_within_zone_tests.csv records four studies for Deciduous forest (n = 811) against the five that actually contribute to it: the fifth, 1114-PF-PDN-DBS-GH-GHANSAH, supplies 4 isolates and falls below the threshold. The zone has five contributing studies and four testable ones, and the two counts are not interchangeable.

[Interpreted] How this is used. Section 7 treats zone and study as inseparable for the four non-overlapping pairs and states which headline numbers are affected. This applies to the marker panel as much as to the genome-wide results: the zone contrasts in Section 6, including pfdhps G437 at 0.964 in Deciduous forest against 0.875 in Guinea savanna, and pfaat1 S258L at 0.824 in Coastal savanna against 0.544 in Guinea savanna, rest on the same zone labels and inherit the same confounding. The zones are named for S258L rather than carried over elliptically, because its Deciduous forest value is 0.735, not 0.824 (results/codon_panel_residues.csv, pfaat1 Ser258Leu: Coastal savanna 0.8235, Deciduous forest 0.7351, Guinea savanna 0.5440, Transitional 0.640, n = 1,896, chi-squared 85.64, p = 1.9 × 10⁻¹⁸). The diagnostic cannot be turned into an adjustment: with no overlap there is no stratum in which to estimate a batch effect and a zone effect simultaneously. What it can do is bound the claim, and that is what it is used for. Stage 13 does not touch this: all six of its scenarios use the same zone labels drawn from the same studies, so none of them is a check on the confounding (Section 3.14).

python step11_batch_confounding.py

3.13 Reproducibility of the corrected re-run

Stages 4 to 9 were re-run end to end with the corrections above in place. Two further changes were needed to make that re-run trustworthy, and both are properly part of the methodology.

Stale-output guards. Stages 5b, 7b and 8b skipped their work if their output files already existed. Those guards did not check whether the input had changed. When the SNP set moved from 3,359 to 3,916, every stage regenerated its inputs, and then ADMIXTURE and TreeMix found their previous output files in place, skipped entirely, and reported success. Only Stage 8 caught the problem, because step08c asserts that its position table and the BayPass output have matching row counts and failed loudly with the correct diagnosis — "Position table has 3,916 rows but the BayPass output has 3,359"; ADMIXTURE and TreeMix carried no such assertion and failed silently. All three guards now require the output to be newer than its input. Had file timestamps not been checked by hand, the previous day's ADMIXTURE and TreeMix results would have been reported as though they came from the new matrix. This is the seventh defect in the chronological sequence this manuscript reports, and it is the one the re-run found by tripping over it rather than one the adversarial audit raised. It is no longer, however, the only defect from outside the audit, and that uniqueness framing is withdrawn: Addendum 3 adds four further defects in the analysis code, raised by the external critique, so the manuscript now reports eleven code defects, five of them raised by something other than the audit. The Introduction carries the post-Addendum 3 count, and the ordinal here is chronological position, not a claim about provenance being unique.

Determinism. After these changes the pipeline has no unseeded stochastic component in the inferential path: BayPass is single-threaded with a fixed seed (step08b_baypass.sh:37, NTHREADS="${NTHREADS:-1}" and -seed "$SEED"), the Mantel permutations are drawn from a seeded generator (step06_mantel.py:176, np.random.default_rng(C.RANDOM_SEED), seed 42 as recorded in mantel_result_*.json), TreeMix's jackknife is deterministic given its block size and is invoked with -k "$BLOCK" -seed "$SEED", and ADMIXTURE was already seeded (step05b_admixture.sh:96, -s "${SEED:-42}"). LD pruning is a different case and an earlier version of this sentence misstated it: plink --indep-pairwise (step05b_admixture.sh:65) is passed no seed and needs none — it has no stochastic component at all, so it is deterministic by construction rather than by seeding. The ordination stage retains its own seeds for UMAP and t-SNE, which are presentational rather than inferential. Stage 12 has no stochastic component at all, and Stage 13 reuses the Stage 6 permutation procedure with the same seed in all six scenarios, which is part of why the scenarios are comparable (Section 3.14).

What Stage 12 did and did not disturb. Stage 12 was written after the corrected re-run, in response to the external critique, and it touches nothing upstream: it fetches its own genotypes, writes three new files, and modifies no existing output. No FST, ADMIXTURE, Mantel, TreeMix, BayPass or annotation result changes because of it. What changes is every marker number in the manuscript, because Stage 10's panel is withdrawn in full rather than adjusted — pfdhps A437G enters at 0.897 where it had been absent, the pfdhfr triple mutant falls from a quoted 0.786–0.908 range to a measured 0.771, the pfcrt 75 "naming discrepancy" is withdrawn as a misdiagnosis, the pfk13 panel is rebuilt from WHO v1.0 and reported with per-codon denominators and a 2009–2021 sampling window, and marginal-frequency validation is replaced by 47,012 of 47,012 per-sample agreements. Section 6's marker tables are built from results/codon_panel_residues.csv, results/codon_panel_haplotypes.csv and results/codon_panel_concordance.csv, and from nothing else.

3.13.1 Two incidents during the final checks

[Observed] A results file was destroyed and restored. results/baypass/xtx_all_snps.csv was found at 0 bytes, truncated at 08:29:53 — hours after the 01:42 run that produced it. It was the only zero-byte file under results/ or interim/. It was regenerated from the intact BayPass outputs, and the regeneration is consistent with the surviving outputs rather than proven row-for-row: results/baypass/outlier_snps.bed, xtx_calibration.json and xtx_threshold.txt are byte-identical by md5 before and after, and the restored file carries the same 3,916 rows and the same 19 outliers. The per-SNP q-values in the restored file also reproduce the calibration counts exactly (Section 3.11).

[Hypothesized] A stray shell redirect during a verification pass — a > where a read was intended — truncated the file. The gap between the 01:42 run and the 08:29:53 truncation rules out the run itself, and that a single file was affected rules out anything systematic, but no command history establishes it.

What the near-loss shows. A results file was destroyed by a command that had nothing to do with it, and nothing in the project noticed. What made the loss recoverable was not a safeguard but an accident of dependency structure: the file is downstream of outputs that survived, so it could be rebuilt, and the surviving md5s make the rebuild checkable. Had the truncated file been an upstream artefact — the genotype matrix, say, or the BayPass core-model output — the same mistake would have cost a re-run at best. The lesson is the same one as the stale-output guards above: the pipeline's integrity rested on files being present and plausible, not on their being verified, and the two are different things.

[Observed] A log misreported its own output path. The --suffix patch to step03_umap.py (Section 3.4.1) suffixed the file writes but not the print statements, so the run reported writing results/dimred_comparison.csv when it had correctly written results/dimred_comparison_qcset.csv. No data were at risk and the published file was not touched, verified from its modification time and contents. The defect was in the log, not in the data. It is fixed.

Why a wrong log line is worth recording. The data were right and the log was wrong, which is the less alarming of the two orderings and still a reproducibility defect: the log is the only record of what a run did, so a run that misstates its own output path invites exactly the misattribution — published figure read as analysis-set figure, or the reverse — that the _qcset suffix was introduced to prevent. It belongs in the same family as the stale-output guards and the truncated results file: three cases in this project where the artefacts were fine and the bookkeeping about them was not.

Robustness. The corrected re-run changed the SNP panel by 16.6 per cent, replaced the Mantel significance test, added false-discovery control to the outlier scan and put the TreeMix model choice on a testable footing without settling the edge count, the two selection criteria still disagreeing. None of the substantive population-genetic conclusions moved. Pairwise FST remained of order 0.002, the ADMIXTURE cross-validation curve remained nearly flat with a negligible improvement at K = 2 (0.84384 at K = 1 against 0.83925 at K = 2, all eight values on the same 3,385-SNP pruned panel — 0.54 per cent), and near-panmixia continued to hold. That is a robustness result in its own right and is reported as one in Section 6.9. It must not, however, be generalised, and the later threshold sensitivity analysis is why: when the two thresholds that define the dataset were varied rather than the filter order, one conclusion did move, and the isolation-by-distance result lost its support (Section 3.14). The re-run's stability was stability under a correction to how the published thresholds were applied, not stability under a change to the thresholds themselves, and only the second kind of check could have found what the second kind of check found. The marker panel is the third case and should not borrow either reassurance: there, the rebuild changed what is reported, and the honest summary is that four rounds of correction each contained a further defect, every one of them a failure of external correctness that internal consistency checking was never going to catch.

3.14 Stage 13 — sensitivity of the results to the two thresholds that define the dataset

What and why. Two thresholds define the analysis dataset and neither is derived from these data. The Fws ≥ 0.95 monoclonality cut (config.py MIN_FWS) is a convention taken from the literature, and it discards 57 per cent of the release-QC-passing isolates, unevenly by zone. The MAF ≥ 0.05 floor (config.py MIN_MAF) is specified in the thesis workflow, and it discards 99.8 per cent of the candidate sites: 3,916 of 1,807,331 survive it. Every headline number in this manuscript rests on both, and until this stage neither had been varied. The audit named that as the most-needed outstanding check. It is now run, and it does not return the answer the manuscript wanted.

Method: one genotype load, six scenarios rebuilt in memory. The 2.19 M × 4,682 genotype matrix is read once. For each scenario the analysis matrix is then rebuilt in memory from that single load under the scenario's thresholds — the isolate set re-derived under the Fws cut, variant missingness recomputed on the isolates that remain, and the MAF floor applied last, in the corrected two-pass order of Section 3.7.1 — and two things are recomputed on the result: pairwise Hudson FST with the same block jackknife as Stage 4b, and the region-level Mantel test with the same seeded 9,999-permutation one-sided procedure and the same censoring treatment as Stage 6. Everything else is held fixed: the depth floor, the mixed-call rule, the sample-missingness bound, clonal pruning, the call-rate and heterozygosity checks, the zone labels and the region centroids. That the no-Fws scenario retains 4,403 isolates rather than all 4,682 in the loaded matrix is the evidence that the other per-isolate filters are still in force.

Why that is cheaper and more comparable than six pipeline re-runs. Cheaper, because the dominant costs of Stages 3a and 4a are the Zarr transfer (299.8 GB over 3.20 hours, Section 3.5) and the genome-wide pass over 2.19 M sites, and both are paid once rather than six times, with no second 20.57 GB local store and no second three-hour transfer window in which a request could fail. More comparable, because all six scenarios are built from the same loaded genotypes, by the same code path, with the same estimator and the same permutation seed, so a difference between two scenarios is attributable to the thresholds and to nothing else. Six separate pipeline runs would have differed in more than the quantity being varied — re-retrieval, column ordering, independently seeded permutation draws — and would have had to be compared across six logs rather than read off one table. The design is also what makes the uniformity of FST below a real finding rather than six coincidences.

Table S13. The six threshold scenarios, from results/sensitivity_summary.csv. Maximum pairwise FST is the Coastal savanna against Guinea savanna pair in all six. Mantel values are the censored region-level test; the uncensored p-values in the same file are 0.0413, 0.1214, 0.2002, 0.1792, 0.0644 and 0.0502. One verdict does turn on the censoring: the MAF ≥ 0.02 scenario is significant censored (0.0467) and not significant uncensored (0.0502), so on the uncensored statistic the baseline is the only scenario clearing 0.05. The other five verdicts are unchanged. The table is numbered after Table S10 so that the existing table numbers do not shift.

[Observed] Isolation by distance is significant in 2 of the 6 scenarios. It holds at the published thresholds (p = 0.0252) and at MAF ≥ 0.02 (p = 0.0467, which is marginal), and it fails at every relaxation of the Fws cut (p = 0.0874, 0.1687, 0.1792) and at MAF ≥ 0.01 (p = 0.0617). The correlation itself does not vanish — r stays between 0.245 and 0.434 — but it stops clearing the threshold, and under the Fws relaxations it also falls.

[Observed] The leave-one-region-out jackknife points the same way. In results/mantel_jackknife_region.csv (md5 779b9e379b6640afbbeb39409206ed69), 4 of the 7 replicates retain p < 0.05 — dropping Volta (r = 0.546, p = 0.0097), Ashanti (r = 0.568, p = 0.014), Brong Ahafo (r = 0.609, p = 0.0398) and Central (r = 0.396, p = 0.0427) — and the same four are the only four below p = 0.10, so the count does not depend on which of those two levels is used. The replicate the audit flagged is dropping Upper East: r falls to 0.260 at p = 0.218. The three non-significant replicates are dropping Eastern (r = 0.288, p = 0.1355), Upper East (r = 0.260, p = 0.218) and Greater Accra (r = 0.250, p = 0.2324). An earlier version of this paragraph quoted p-values of 0.0048, 0.0155, 0.0543, 0.0645 and 0.3307; those came from the superseded 3,359-SNP run, match no current output file, and are corrected here against the live result.

[Interpreted] The isolation-by-distance finding must therefore be reported as not robust. It holds at the published thresholds and almost nowhere else: 2 of 6 threshold scenarios, 4 of 7 jackknife replicates, and it collapses when a single region is removed. Any grading of it as "supported", "moderately supported", "a weak but real distance effect" or similar is withdrawn wherever it appears in this manuscript. This is a loss of a result, and the manuscript's only positive spatial finding at that — it is not a caveat attached to a surviving conclusion. The result is not deleted: the baseline estimate and its p-value stand as computed, and they are reported alongside the five scenarios in which the test does not clear 0.05, so that the reader sees the whole of what varying the thresholds does. What cannot be said is that the data show isolation by distance.

[Observed] Near-panmixia, by contrast, is robust, and this strengthens it. Maximum pairwise FST stays within 0.00204 to 0.00255 across all six scenarios — a total spread of 0.0005 — over isolate counts from 1,900 to 4,403 and SNP counts from 3,275 to 10,876. The headline conclusion is insensitive to both thresholds, across more than a doubling of the sample and nearly a trebling of the panel, and that is the strongest single piece of evidence for it anywhere in this analysis.

Relaxing Fws slightly raises FST (0.00232 at the published cut to 0.00255 at Fws ≥ 0.80 and 0.00254 with no cut at all). Polyclonal isolates were therefore not masking differentiation — the objection that the monoclonality filter might have thrown away the signal runs the wrong way.

Tripling the SNP count via MAF ≥ 0.01 (3,916 to 10,876 sites) lowers FST to 0.00204, as expected if rarer variants are less differentiated between zones. The published MAF floor is therefore, if anything, mildly anticonservative with respect to the panmixia conclusion, and the conclusion survives the correction.

Why one conclusion strengthens while the other falls. The two outcomes are not in tension, and the asymmetry is instructive rather than suspicious. Maximum FST is a point estimate pooled over thousands of sites, so it moves little when the site set or the sample changes, and six scenarios agreeing to within 0.0005 is a genuine stability result. The Mantel p-value is a significance verdict on a correlation over 21 pairwise points from seven populations, and at baseline it sat at 0.0252 — inside the top half of the interval below 0.05, with very little margin. A verdict with that little margin, resting on that few points, is exactly the kind that threshold choices can decide, and here they do. The honest reading is that the panmixia estimate was always well determined and the distance effect never was; what the check did was reveal which of the two the published thresholds had been carrying.

What this check does not do. It varies two thresholds and nothing else. It does not vary the zone labels, the admin-1 centroid coordinates (all six scenarios share the same seven coordinate pairs, so none addresses the resolution limit of Section 3.3), the great-circle distance metric, the log-distance model, or the study confounding of Section 3.12 — all six scenarios draw on the same studies in the same zones, so the four non-overlapping zone pairs are confounded in every one of them. The six scenarios are also not independent: they share isolates and sites, so "2 of 6" is a description of the scenario grid and not a probability. And the two thresholds are varied one at a time from the baseline, never jointly.

[Proposed] Cross the two thresholds rather than varying them singly — the Fws relaxations at MAF ≥ 0.01 and 0.02, and the MAF relaxations with no Fws cut. The machinery now exists and one genotype load covers the whole grid, so the marginal cost is small. It would show whether the two thresholds interact, which the present design cannot see.

python step13_sensitivity.py



4. Filtering and inclusion/exclusion criteria

This section documents every filter applied, with counts before and after, and states for each whether it was specified in advance (in the thesis workflow or the supplied pipeline), chosen during this session on the basis of exploratory output, or introduced retrospectively. All variant-level counts from Stage 4a onward — the genotype depth mask, mixed-call resolution, variant missingness, MAF and LD pruning — come from the corrected re-run of 2026-10-10 (logs/rerun_corrected.log), which fixed the order in which variant missingness and the sample cut are applied (Section 4.2.7). The Stage 3a site counts in row 4 of Table S14 (12,493,205 total variants and 2,190,026 PASS biallelic SNPs) are the one exception: they come from logs/step03a_fetch_callset.log and do not appear in the re-run log, because Stage 3a was not re-run (see the provenance caveat below). The sample-level counts are unchanged from the original run; the variant-level counts are not, and any variant-level figure quoted in an earlier draft of this manuscript is superseded.

4.1 The filtering cascade

Table S14. Complete filtering cascade, corrected run of 2026-10-10. Rows are in execution order; the # column keeps each filter's original identifier, so filters 12 and 13 now appear in the reverse of their numerical order (see Section 4.2.7). Isolate-level filters 5–9 are not sequential — each is evaluated independently against the same 4,905 isolates and an isolate may fail several — so before/after is shown as "—" for all five and only their combined effect has a meaningful pair. The QC summary in logs/step03b_sample_qc.log confirms the flags are independent (fail_call_rate 20, fail_call_rate_outlier 0, fail_het_outlier 126, fail_fws 2,810, fail_clonal 77, all against 4,905). Filter 6 was disabled, so no isolate set was ever evaluated at that position and its zero is the count with the rule off; had it been enabled, 1,281 isolates fall below its bound and 451 of those fail no other check (Section 4.2.3). Sources: logs/step01_filter_samples.log, logs/step03a_fetch_callset.log (row 4), logs/step03b_sample_qc.log, logs/rerun_corrected.log, results/sample_qc_full.csv.

Scope caveat on row 10. Two distinct quantities are involved in the depth mask and they must not be subtracted from one another: 3,709,090,355 genotype cells fail DP < 5 across the full 2,190,026 × 6,653 array, most of which carried no call to begin with; of these, 1,695,066,787 carried a call, taking the called-genotype total from 12,540,727,880 to 10,845,661,093 — 13.52 per cent of called genotypes masked. Section 6.1 states the same two facts in the same form, and no other formulation should appear anywhere in this manuscript. The mask runs before sample alignment, so 13.52 per cent is computed over all 6,653 fetched Ghanaian isolates, whereas rows 11–14 are over the 2,017 that passed QC: one row of the cascade uses a denominator about 3.3 times larger than its neighbours. Whether the equivalent rate on the isolates that actually entered the analysis is higher or lower than 13.52 per cent is [Unknown], and an earlier draft's claim that the table overstates the mask's cost is withdrawn. Nothing measured establishes a direction: the QC panel's call rate separates the two groups by 0.0012 (median 0.9968 over the 2,017 retained against 0.9956 over the 2,888 discarded; the 0.9962 in the log is the median over all 4,905 zone-assigned isolates, not the retained subset), 1,748 of the isolates cut at filter 2 have no call-rate row in results/sample_qc_full.csv at all, and that panel is in any case computed over 50,000 SNPs with no DP or GQ mask applied, so it does not measure what the depth floor removes — its 0.996 sits roughly 13.5 points above the genome-wide calledness the mask was computed on. Stage 4a does not print the rate again after alignment, so the aligned-scope figure is not available.

Provenance caveat on row 4. The 12,493,205 total-variant and 2,190,026 PASS-biallelic counts come from logs/step03a_fetch_callset.log, which was not re-run; Stage 3a is unchanged by this revision, and neither figure appears in logs/rerun_corrected.log. The 2,190,026 figure was independently confirmed by the audit against the public Pf8 Zarr store. The numalt = 1 clause in this row is the filter that silently removed most canonical antimalarial-resistance codons — 28 of the 44 codon-allele records genotyped, among them pfcrt K76T, pfmdr1 N86Y, pfdhps K540E and 8 of the 14 validated pfk13 codons; of the 21 records that are polymorphic in this sample, only 6 survived the filter from the genome-wide callset — see Section 6 and the separate multiallelic-aware marker panel, which bypasses it.



Figure S7. Sample-level QC distributions over the 4,905 zone-assigned Ghanaian isolates: call rate, heterozygous call rate and Fws, with the applied thresholds marked. Source: pf8_proof/sample_qc.png, the copy that ships with this manuscript.

Figure provenance. The sample-level stage was not re-run — logs/step03b_sample_qc.log is dated 9 October, before the 10 October corrected re-run — so this is the same figure as in the original run and no regenerated version exists or is required. The shipping asset is pf8_proof/sample_qc.png (519,880 bytes); the server copy at figures/sample_qc.png is 519,864 bytes. The two are not byte-identical, which is why the figure is identified here by the path that travels with the manuscript rather than by the server path.

4.2 Rationale, origin and consequences of each non-trivial filter

4.2.1 Pf8 QC-pass flag (filter 2)

Purpose and origin. Pre-specified. Removes isolates MalariaGEN itself judged unreliable, on the basis of sequencing-run information not available downstream.

[Observed] Effect. 1,748 of 6,653 isolates removed (26.3 per cent). The post-QC regional counts are Upper East 3,605, Greater Accra 435, Ashanti 391, Central 309, Eastern 74, Brong Ahafo 50 and Volta 41, read from logs/step01_filter_samples.log for this revision.

[Calculated] The loss is not uniform across regions: Central loses 55.0 per cent (686 → 309) and Upper East 25.2 per cent (4,821 → 3,605), while Volta loses none (41 → 41). These percentages are not in logs/step01_filter_samples.log, which prints the admin-1 breakdown only downstream of the QC filter and therefore carries post-QC counts alone; they were recomputed from the Pf8 manifest (data/Pf8_samples.txt) for this revision.

[Interpreted] The earlier draft labelled differential regional loss as most likely reflecting collection, storage or sequencing-batch differences, and said the manifest did not carry the fields needed to confirm it. That is no longer the right status. The manifest does carry the partner-study field, and the study-by-zone crosstab in Section 4.3 shows that zone in Ghana is very nearly a relabelling of sequencing study. Differential regional loss is therefore consistent with — and most parsimoniously explained by — study-level heterogeneity, which is now quantified rather than hypothesised. What remains untested is whether any of it is biological.

4.2.2 Fws ≥ 0.95 (filter 8)

Purpose. To exclude polyclonal infections. A mixed infection contributes allele frequencies from several parasite genomes to a single genotype column; ADMIXTURE reads that as intermediate ancestry, so an uncorrected analysis risks describing the transmission-intensity gradient rather than the ancestry structure it is meant to estimate.

[Observed] Origin. Pre-specified. 0.95 is the conventional cut and is the pipeline default (config.MIN_FWS). It was re-confirmed during this session against the observed Fws distribution (Figure S7, third panel), which shows a pronounced mode above 0.95 and a long left shoulder, so the threshold falls at a natural break rather than cutting through a dense region. Median Fws over the 4,905 isolates is 0.895 (range 0.310–1.000); 2,095 of 4,905 (42.7 per cent) are monoclonal at the cut.

[Observed] Effect and its most important consequence. 2,810 of 4,905 isolates removed — by far the largest single exclusion. The polyclonal fraction differs by zone: Guinea savanna 0.597 (of 3,605 isolates), deciduous forest 0.539 (of 815), transitional zone 0.500 (of 50) and coastal savanna 0.444 (of 435). These fractions, like every study-composition and within-zone batch figure in this manuscript, are computed on the 4,905 QC-passing isolates — the set on which study membership and Fws are defined — and not on the 1,900-isolate analysis set, whose zone counts are Guinea savanna 1,334, deciduous forest 337, coastal savanna 204 and transitional zone 25. The denominator is stated in full in Section 2.6 and applies wherever these numbers appear (Sections 4.3, 6.2, 7.2, 7.3 and 10.1).

[Interpreted] Higher transmission produces more mixed infections, so this ordering is itself a transmission signal, and the filter removes a greater share of isolates from the high-transmission Guinea savanna than from the coast. It is not, however, a clean south-to-north cline, and should not be reported as one: the transitional zone is the second-most-northern of the four zones yet sits third of four on polyclonal fraction, below deciduous forest, so the ordering is Guinea savanna > deciduous forest > transitional zone > coastal savanna rather than monotonic in latitude — and the transitional value rests on 50 isolates drawn from a single study. What can be said without qualification is that the retained sample is not a random subsample of the Ghanaian parasite population, and that the degree of non-randomness differs by zone. Because the filter targets monoclonality rather than any allele, the direct effect on allele frequencies should be modest, but this has not been tested.

[Observed] The zone ordering of the polyclonal fraction is not separable from sequencing study. Within deciduous forest, the four studies large enough to test — of five contributing; the fifth has 4 isolates — differ in Fws (Kruskal–Wallis H = 14.9, p = 1.9 × 10⁻³, n = 811), and their polyclonal fractions span 0.430 to 0.578, which is most of the between-zone range. The p-value is a test on the Fws distributions, not on the polyclonal fraction itself; no test of the fraction exists in the outputs, and the two should not be conflated. The zone's composition is five studies (815 isolates); only the test is over four. See Section 4.3.

[Proposed] Re-run the FST and ADMIXTURE analyses at Fws ≥ 0.90 and ≥ 0.80 and compare. If conclusions are unchanged, the sensitivity concern is discharged cheaply. The retained-isolate projections quoted for these thresholds in the earlier draft are withdrawn: they could not be reproduced from any committed script, and an independent recomputation from results/sample_qc_full.csv disagreed with both by two isolates.

4.2.3 Call-rate outlier rule (filter 6) — disabled

[Observed] What the pipeline did. A median-absolute-deviation rule flags isolates whose call rate falls more than three scaled MADs below the median. Because Ghanaian call rates are tightly bunched near 1 (median 0.9962 over the 4,905 zone-assigned isolates, scaled MAD 0.004833), the resulting bound sits at 0.98174 and flagged 1,281 isolates.

Decision and origin. Session decision, taken on exploratory output. The rule was disabled, leaving only the absolute floor of 0.80 (config.MIN_SAMPLE_CALL_RATE, pre-specified). The reasoning: the MAD-flagged, otherwise-passing isolates had a median call rate of 0.9551 and a range of 0.803–0.982, which is not poor data in absolute terms, and Stage 4a applies an independent sample-missingness bound later in any case. A MAD rule is designed to find outliers in a dispersed distribution; applied to a tight one it becomes an arbitrary strict threshold.

Effect — restated. The earlier draft read: "Retaining these isolates raised the QC-passing count from 1,566 to 2,017 and the final analysis set from 1,862 to 1,900." The second half of that sentence was misattributed, and the two halves could not both be true: the final analysis set is a subset of the QC-passing set (filter 13 runs 2,017 → 1,900), so a QC-passing count of 1,566 cannot produce a final set of 1,862.

[Calculated] The first half is correct and is now reproducible. Re-applying the MAD rule to results/sample_qc_full.csv at its own bound of 0.98174 flags 1,281 isolates, of which 451 fail no other check. Enabling the rule would leave 1,566 QC-passing isolates instead of 2,017 — per zone, Guinea savanna 1,115, deciduous forest 272, coastal savanna 154, transitional zone 25. This was recomputed from the primary QC table for this revision; it is not printed in any log, which is why the audit could not locate it.

[Observed] The second half belongs to a different decision. The 1,862-isolate final set comes from logs/run_stages_4to7.log, and that run already had the MAD rule disabled — it used the same 2,017 QC-passing isolates. The only difference from the run that produced 1,900 was the genotype mask: GQ < 20 enabled (4,706,404,099 calls below GQ 20; 12,540,727,880 → 9,863,685,827, 21.35 per cent of calls masked; final matrix 2,143 SNPs × 1,862 isolates) versus DP-only (13.52 per cent masked; 3,359 SNPs × 1,900 isolates). Both of those matrices are from the superseded filter order.

[Calculated] It follows that the 1,862 → 1,900 change measures the genotype-quality decision of Section 4.2.5, counted a second time in the earlier draft under the wrong heading. That reading is an inference about the earlier draft's bookkeeping rather than a logged quantity, but it is forced by the two runs' settings; the claim is removed from this subsection.

[Unknown] What the call-rate decision cost or bought at the level of the final analysis matrix is therefore not known. Establishing it requires re-running Stage 4a with the MAD rule enabled, under the current DP-only mask and the corrected filter order. That has not been done, and no replacement figure is offered here. The benefit claimed for this decision in the earlier draft was the benefit of a different decision.

Table S15. What is and is not established about the call-rate decision. This table replaces the projected sensitivity table of the earlier draft (rows "MAD rule on" / "MAD rule off" with projected SNP counts of 2,117 and 3,416). Those projections were withdrawn for two reasons: the audit could not reproduce any of their cells from a committed script or log, and the quantity they projected — the final SNP count — has since changed with the corrected filter order, so they could not be compared against the current run even if their provenance were sound.

Honesty note. This threshold was chosen after seeing its effect on sample counts, not in advance. The justification above is a post-hoc rationalisation of a decision made during exploratory analysis. It is defensible, but it was not pre-registered, and a reviewer is entitled to ask whether the alternative was examined with equal care. The earlier draft pointed to Table S15 as evidence that the alternatives had been quantified before the choice was made; that claim cannot be supported, because the projections in question are not reproducible. What can be said is narrower: the alternative's effect on the QC-passing count is now known (1,566 against 2,017) and its effect on the final matrix is not.

4.2.4 Clonal pruning threshold (filter 9)

Problem. Unlike Fws, the clonality threshold has no conventional value. The pipeline's own histogram heuristic suggested 0.00005, and the log prints that suggestion together with an explicit warning that it is a heuristic. The earlier draft said the data "offer no clean break"; that overstates the case, since the pipeline's histogram did identify a trough and the number of isolates removed rises smoothly only once the threshold is pushed well past it.

Table S16. Sensitivity of clonal pruning to the distance threshold, computed on the cached 4,905 × 4,905 Ghanaian distance submatrix. At the chosen threshold the pipeline reports 68 clonal groups spanning 145 isolates, of which 77 are dropped (logs/step03b_sample_qc.log). The pairwise distance distribution between Ghanaian isolates runs from a minimum of 0.00000 through a 1st percentile of 0.00090 to a median of 0.00112, so even the loosest threshold shown is well inside the left tail. The audit independently reproduced all 35 cells of this table from interim/ghana_dist_submatrix.npy; the corresponding script is not part of the pipeline.

Decision and origin. Session decision. 0.00005 was chosen: isolates within about 4 per cent of the typical pairwise distance, which is a defensible reading of "essentially the same genome". The largest connected component at this threshold contains 3 isolates, so single-linkage chaining — the real danger of this method, where a sequence of moderately similar isolates merges into one large component — is not occurring. At 0.0007 the largest component reaches 30 isolates and chaining is clearly setting in.

[Observed] Effect. 77 isolates removed, 1.6 per cent of the 4,905 zone-assigned set. Because the effect is small and the method is well behaved at this threshold, sensitivity to the exact value is unlikely to be material — but this has not been verified by re-running downstream analyses.

4.2.5 Genotype quality mask (filter 10)

[Observed] Problem. The pipeline default masked calls below DP 5 or GQ 20. Under that default the final matrix held 2,143 SNPs over 1,862 isolates; removing the GQ component raised it to 3,359 SNPs over 1,900 isolates. Both figures are from the superseded filter order. The corresponding DP+GQ run has not been repeated under the corrected order, so the current 3,916-SNP matrix has no like-for-like GQ-masked counterpart.

Evidence gathered before deciding. A 40,000-SNP diagnostic panel showed that 12.6 per cent of homozygous-reference and 12.4 per cent of homozygous-alternate calls fall below GQ 20, with median depths of 46 and 40 respectively — that is, the GQ < 20 population is not a low-depth population. Restricted to calls with DP ≥ 10, only 3.0 per cent of homozygous-reference and 0.7 per cent of homozygous-alternate calls still fail GQ 20. Applying the GQ rule reduced the share of panel SNPs passing the ≤ 10 per cent missingness bar from 74.3 to 51.0 per cent — a relative reduction of 31 per cent, not a halving, as the earlier draft stated — while mean alternate-allele frequency at common SNPs moved from 0.282 to 0.294.

[Unknown] None of the diagnostic-panel figures in the preceding paragraph can be located in any log, script or results file on the server. They were tagged [Observed] in the earlier draft, which the manuscript defines as printed by a pipeline script and preserved in a log; they do not meet that definition. The diagnostic script was never committed. The figures are retained here because they are what the decision was actually taken on, but their provenance is unrecoverable and they should be read as such.

Decision and origin. Session decision, taken on that evidence and confirmed with the user. config.MIN_GENOTYPE_GQ was set to 0, retaining DP ≥ 5. The reasoning: the GQ rule was removing a large number of common SNPs at substantial cost to statistical power, while barely changing allele frequencies — that is, it was costing precision without buying accuracy.

[Interpreted] The earlier draft explained low GQ as an artefact of a haploid organism forced into a diploid calling model, so that intermediate GQ values arise from the model's own ambiguity rather than from poor data. That interpretation is not supported. The audit measured low-GQ call rates by sequencing study and found them differing roughly tenfold between studies within a single zone, which a uniform model artefact cannot explain, since a model artefact should be uniform across studies by construction. The heterogeneity is therefore study-associated; but study also differs in collection period, sample handling and recruitment, so this does not on its own identify a technical batch cause. The decision may still be the right one, but this is no longer its reason.

Honesty note — the GQ decision is still unvalidated. No direct genotype-concordance check has been run, and the decision therefore rests on indirect evidence throughout. The two specific gaps: (i) the allele-frequency comparison offered in support (0.282 against 0.294) is between two different SNP sets, not the same sites under the two masks, so it does not test whether dropping the GQ rule admits wrong calls — only that the two surviving sets have similar mean frequencies; (ii) no comparison has been made against an independent truth set, although one exists and was used elsewhere in this project. MalariaGEN publishes its own genotype calls for these same 1,900 isolates, and they were used successfully to cross-validate the resistance-marker panel (47,012 of 47,012 per-sample calls agreeing, across the 25 of the panel's 57 codons for which MalariaGEN publishes a per-sample genotype). The equivalent check here — per-call concordance between DP-only and DP+GQ masking at a common set of sites, benchmarked against those published calls — is cheap, is the check the manuscript's own reviewer note asks for first, and has not been done. Until it is, the GQ decision should be read as a power-for-accuracy trade made on plausible but indirect grounds, and the SNP-count gain it produced — 3,359 against 2,143, about 57 per cent, both under the superseded filter order — should be reported as contingent on it.

4.2.6 MAF ≥ 5 per cent (filter 14)

Origin. Pre-specified in the thesis workflow ("MAF > 5%") and not altered.

[Observed] Effect — the most consequential number in the analysis. 1,807,331 SNPs pass the recomputed missingness filter; 3,916 pass MAF. This is a reduction of 99.78 per cent. Under the superseded filter order the same step ran on 1,634,808 variants and yielded 3,359, so the corrected order adds 557 SNPs (+16.6 per cent) at an identical threshold — see Section 4.2.7.

[Interpreted] P. falciparum carries an extremely large number of rare variants and comparatively few common ones within a single country. A 5 per cent MAF filter in this setting is far more aggressive than the same nominal threshold would be in, say, a human cohort. Every downstream analysis — FST, ADMIXTURE, TreeMix, BayPass — operates on these 3,916 sites (3,385 after LD pruning for ADMIXTURE), and the BayPass scan in particular has limited power at this density: 19 SNPs clear the POD quantile threshold and 13 of those also survive FDR 10 per cent.

[Unknown] The earlier draft supported this paragraph with a stated genome-wide ceiling of roughly 5,500 common SNPs ("about 4,832 SNPs" with missingness ≤ 10 per cent and no genotype masking, "about 5,529" ignoring missingness), and concluded that the analysis retained 3,359 of them. Neither figure can be found in any log, script or results file, and the diagnostic that produced them was never committed. The ceiling claim is withdrawn rather than updated: with no reproducible denominator, the fraction of the available common-variant panel that this analysis sees is not known. What is known is that correcting the filter order alone recovered 557 SNPs, which indicates the earlier figures were not a hard ceiling.

Status of the 4,832 and 5,529 ceiling figures elsewhere in the manuscript. Both were computed on a diagnostic SNP panel under the DP-only mask and against the superseded filter order, before the re-run: they are pre-re-run projections, not measurements of the current callset, and they should be labelled as such wherever they appear. The roughly 5,500-SNP ceiling is carried into Section 7.3, the limitations table in Section 9 and the threshold-stability item in Section 10.3, all of which inherited the 3,359 denominator. The retained count to state against it is 3,916 — about 71 per cent of the figure as quoted — not 3,359. The position taken above is the honest one and should govern: the ceiling is a withdrawn projection rather than a bound, and any section that still quotes it must say so.

[Proposed] Repeat the main analyses at MAF ≥ 1 per cent as a sensitivity check. This departs from the thesis specification and so should be reported as a robustness analysis rather than as the primary result, but the gain in power for the outlier scan could be substantial. The projected SNP range quoted for this in the earlier draft is withdrawn for the same provenance reason; the projection should be redone from the current callset and printed to a log.

4.2.7 Filter order: variant missingness after the sample cut (filters 12 and 13)

Problem. Stage 4a measured per-variant missingness against all 2,017 QC-passing isolates, applied the ≤ 0.10 bar, and only then dropped 117 isolates for excess sample missingness. Every SNP was therefore charged for missing calls in isolates that did not survive to be analysed. The 117 dropped isolates are not a distinct bad-data population but the lower tail of the same call-rate distribution (call rates 0.803–0.989, median 0.882, against a retained minimum of 0.896), so they inflated every SNP's measured missingness. This is the conventional --mind before --geno ordering in PLINK, and the pipeline had it the other way round. The earlier draft described the filter order as deliberate; that was only half right — masking before missingness is correct, but measuring variant missingness before the sample drop was not.

Fix. Stage 4a is now two-pass. A provisional variant set is computed exactly as before, over the 2,017 QC-passing isolates, and is used only to decide the sample cut — so the same 117 isolates drop and the sample set is unchanged. Variant missingness is then recomputed on the 1,900 isolates that remain, and MAF is applied to that set. Thresholds are identical, the sample set is identical, and the provisional subset is never materialised, the pass being implemented blockwise.

provisional variant pass (for the sample cut): 1,634,808

dropping 117 high-missingness samples

variant missingness <= 0.1 (recomputed on 1,900 retained isolates): 1,807,331

MAF >= 0.05: 3,916

Table S17. Effect of correcting the filter order. Both columns use the same thresholds, the same genotype mask (DP ≥ 5) and the same 1,900 isolates; the only change is that variant missingness is measured after the sample cut rather than before it. Sources: logs/run_from4_parallel.log (original), logs/rerun_corrected.log (corrected).

[Observed] Effect. 1,807,331 variants pass the recomputed missingness bar against 1,634,808 under the original order, a pass-rate increase from 74.65 to 82.53 per cent of the 2,190,026 PASS biallelic sites. After MAF the final matrix holds 3,916 SNPs rather than 3,359: 557 more sites, a 16.6 per cent increase, on the identical 1,900 isolates, at no change to any threshold.

[Interpreted] The audit projected this gain at roughly +344 SNPs (to about 3,703) from a 50,000-SNP calibration panel. The realised gain is larger, which is consistent with the panel being an unbiased but noisy estimator of a genome-wide pass rate rather than with any discrepancy in the fix itself: on the 2,017-isolate denominator the panel put the missingness pass rate at 74.2 per cent against the pipeline's genome-wide 74.65 per cent, reproducing it to within half a percentage point.

Origin. Introduced retrospectively, in this revision, following the audit. It is a departure from the pipeline as supplied and is recorded as such. It is also the only change in this revision that alters the size of the analysis matrix, and therefore the one that propagates into every downstream number: the FST matrix, the ADMIXTURE cross-validation curve, the TreeMix sweep and the BayPass scan were all re-run on the 3,916-SNP matrix.

Why this matters more as a robustness result than as a correction. A 16.6 per cent larger common-variant panel did not move any conclusion. Maximum pairwise FST stayed at 0.0023 against a previously reported 0.0024; the ADMIXTURE cross-validation curve remains near-flat with a negligible K = 2 improvement over K = 1 (0.83925 against 0.84384); the outlier scan's top signals are the same genes. The substantive reading of this is not that the earlier numbers were wrong in any consequential way, but that the near-panmixia result is insensitive to a substantial change in panel size — which is the most useful form of reassurance available for a result of this kind, and is reported in Section 10 as such. The correction is still required: the earlier numbers were the output of a defective ordering, and that has to be stated whether or not it changed the answer.

Caveat. One further defect surfaced during the re-run itself and is recorded here because it bears directly on the filter cascade. Three downstream stages guarded their work with a "skip if output exists" test that did not check whether the input had changed. When the SNP set went from 3,359 to 3,916, ADMIXTURE and TreeMix regenerated their inputs, found their previous output files in place, skipped the work and reported success; only the BayPass stage caught it, because it asserts that its position table and the BayPass output have matching row counts and failed with that exact diagnosis. All three guards now require the output to be newer than its input. Had the file timestamps not been inspected, the previous day's ADMIXTURE and TreeMix results would have been reported against the new matrix.

4.3 Selection bias summary

Four biases and design confounds are introduced or amplified by the filtering and by the sampling frame, in decreasing order of concern for the between-zone comparisons that carry this analysis.

[Observed] Study and batch confounding (from the sampling frame; a fourth design limit, not listed in the earlier draft). Pf8 isolates arrive through named partner studies, and in Ghana those studies are almost perfectly nested within zones. Four of six zone pairs share no sequencing study at all — including coastal savanna versus Guinea savanna, the pair that produces the maximum pairwise FST and the polyclonality contrast. On the 4,905 QC-passing isolates each zone is dominated by a single study: transitional zone 100 per cent (1083-PF-GH-CONWAY, 50 of 50), coastal savanna 89 per cent (1241-PF-GH-ANINAGYEI, 387 of 435), Guinea savanna 74 per cent (1168-PF-GH-AMENGA-ETEGO-SM, 2,678 of 3,605) and deciduous forest 48 per cent (1200-PF-GH-MAIGA-SM, 391 of 815). Deciduous forest has five contributing studies in total; the fifth, 1114-PF-PDN-DBS-GH-GHANSAH, contributes 4 isolates.

Denominator for the batch figures. The study-composition and within-zone batch tests in this section and in Section 4.2.2 are computed on the 4,905 QC-passing isolates, the set on which study membership and Fws are defined; the population analyses use the 1,900-isolate subset, whose zone counts are Guinea savanna 1,334, deciduous forest 337, coastal savanna 204 and transitional zone 25. The two sets must not be read against one another: "1083-PF-GH-CONWAY is 50 of 50 in the transitional zone" and "transitional zone, n = 25" are statements about different sets, not a contradiction. Study nesting within zone is a property of the Pf8 sampling frame and is not altered by the later cuts, and this was checked rather than assumed: the zone-pair overlap was recomputed on the 1,900-isolate analysis set for this revision and is identical — four of six pairs share no study, coastal savanna and deciduous forest still share two (1183-PF-GH-AWANDARE-SM, 1241-PF-GH-ANINAGYEI) and Guinea savanna and the transitional zone still share one (1083-PF-GH-CONWAY) — with single-study dominance of 100 per cent in the transitional zone (25 of 25), 86.8 per cent coastal savanna (177 of 204), 73.8 per cent Guinea savanna (984 of 1,334) and 46.6 per cent deciduous forest (157 of 337); deciduous forest still draws on five studies. Source: interim/analysis_samples.csv, computed for this revision. The within-zone Kruskal–Wallis tests themselves were not recomputed on the 1,900 set and remain 4,905-set figures.

[Observed] Study-associated heterogeneity here is demonstrable rather than hypothetical. Holding zone constant, so that study varies together with collection year, finer geography and recruitment: within Guinea savanna, call rate differs between its six contributing studies at Kruskal–Wallis p = 2.8 × 10⁻¹⁹⁷; within deciduous forest, at p = 7.7 × 10⁻⁸⁸. In deciduous forest the Fws distributions also differ between the four studies large enough to test, of five contributing (Kruskal–Wallis H = 14.9, p = 1.9 × 10⁻³, n = 811), and their polyclonal fractions span 0.430 to 0.578; the p-value belongs to the Fws test, not to the fraction, for which no test exists in the outputs. For the two zone pairs that do share a study, the within-study comparison reproduces the overall direction: within 1083-PF-GH-CONWAY, Guinea savanna is more polyclonal than the transitional zone (0.532 against 0.500), matching the overall ordering. The gradient is therefore not purely an artefact — it is simply not separable from batch for the pairs that carry the largest differences.

Transmission-intensity bias (from the Fws filter). The retained sample under-represents high-transmission settings, and does so unevenly across zones. Of the biases that the filtering itself introduces, this is the one most likely to affect between-zone comparisons.

Regional bias (from the Pf8 QC flag and from the sampling frame itself). Upper East dominates every stage. No filter corrects this and none was intended to. It is also inseparable from the previous item, since Upper East is largely one study.

Allele-frequency bias (from the MAF filter). The analysis sees only the common end of the frequency spectrum. Rare variants, which is where most recent selection signal would sit, are invisible to it. The numalt = 1 clause on filter 4 compounds this at the loci of greatest applied interest: it removed most canonical antimalarial-resistance codons — 28 of the 44 codon-allele records genotyped, among them pfcrt K76T, pfmdr1 N86Y, pfdhps K540E and 8 of the 14 validated pfk13 codons; of the 21 records that are polymorphic in this sample, only 6 survived the filter from the scanned callset, and those codons had to be recovered by a separate multiallelic-aware panel rather than by relaxing MAF.

[Interpreted] None of the first three is an error; each follows from a defensible choice. The fourth — study confounding — is not a choice at all but a property of the released data, and it is the one that cannot be mitigated by any reanalysis of this sample. Together they mean the final 1,900-isolate, 3,916-SNP dataset is a particular view of Ghanaian P. falciparum — monoclonal infections, common variants, Upper East–heavy, and with zone very nearly a relabelling of sequencing study — and conclusions should be stated as applying to that view. In particular, a between-zone difference in this dataset cannot be attributed to agro-ecology in preference to batch for four of the six zone pairs.

Caveat on the zone layer itself. Every zone-level count in this section depends on ghanaAEZ.geojson, the agro-ecological zone polygons used for the spatial join. That layer has no citable source: it carries no attribution, source, author or date field, and was traced to an anonymous ArcGIS Online re-host that is byte-for-byte identical to the local copy (md5 96b1a7f4da62d6f0153826f41af96152) and whose licence and access-information fields are both null. It is not an origin, and it is a hybrid of two different classification schemes rather than a reproduction of Ghana's canonical MoFA/FAO zones. It should be cited as accessed rather than as authoritative, and the provenance gap stated as a limitation. Zone names are reproduced verbatim throughout this manuscript — including the layer's "Decidous forest" misspelling in table labels and output files — because the spatial join keys on them.



5. Analytical decisions and iterations

This section reconstructs the decisions taken during the analysis. Where a decision was put to the user and answered, that is stated. Where the rationale is reconstructed after the fact, it is labelled as such. The section has now been revised three times: after an adversarial audit of the completed analysis, after the corrected re-run that followed it, and after an external critique of the marker panel whose five points were all upheld and four of which were defects in code rather than in prose. Several decisions recorded here were wrong, and the stated account of why two others worked was wrong. They are corrected in place rather than removed, because the reasoning that produced them is part of what this section is for.

5.1 Replacing the VCF route with direct Zarr retrieval

[Observed] Problem. The project had 14 per-chromosome VCFs in data/, produced by data/extract_ghana.sh, which streamed each Pf8 chromosome VCF from Sanger through bcftools view with a Ghana sample list. Thirteen of the fourteen were truncated. Inspection of the BGZF end-of-file marker and decoding of the last record in each file showed coverage from 9 per cent (chromosome 12) to 53 per cent (chromosome 9); only chromosome 10 was complete.

Cause. bcftools aborted with "[E::bcf_calc_ac] Incorrect AN/AC counts" while recomputing allele counts for the sample subset. Each truncation point matches its recorded error position exactly — chromosome 12 failed at position 200,583 and its last record is at 200,581. The extraction script echoed "Finished" regardless of the exit status, so the failures went unnoticed. The errors are preserved in data/extract_ghana.log and data/chr05_extract.log; the consequent end-of-file warnings are in data/index_tbi.log.

[Observed] Constraint. The one complete VCF, chromosome 10, is 34.7 GB for 1.69 Mb of sequence, against 352 GB free on the data volume.

[Calculated] At that rate the full genome would require approximately 480 GB. A simple re-download was therefore not possible.

Options put to the user.

Table S18. Genotype-retrieval options presented. Estimates for A derive from a direct probe of the Zarr store's chunk grid; B and C are extrapolated from the observed chromosome-10 download.

Decision. Option A, chosen by the user. A fourth implicit option — the project's own step04a_prepare_callset_malariagen.py, which pulls from Google Cloud via the malariagen_data package — was not pursued because it would have required changes to run_all.sh and because Step 3b reads the local store directly.

Consequence and verification. The new script produced a 20.57 GB store verified exactly against three VCF windows (Section 3.5). On that evidence, and with the user's approval, the 13 truncated VCFs and their indexes were deleted, freeing about 125 GB; the complete chromosome-10 VCF was retained as a verification reference.

[Interpreted] Beyond solving the immediate problem, this route is strictly better for the analysis: it removes the AC/AN recomputation that caused the failure, it carries the RegionType annotation that makes the missing core-genome BED unnecessary, and it is reproducible from a public URL rather than from a 160 GB local artefact. It also made the resistance-panel recovery of Section 5.7.1 possible at all, and the second rebuild of Section 5.7.3 after it, because re-fetching nine genes under a different filter from a Zarr store is a minutes-long operation rather than a re-download. The panel has now been re-fetched twice, which only a cheap retrieval path makes practical.

But note. The phrase "PASS biallelic SNPs only" in Option A is the decision that later invalidated a headline conclusion. It stands for two separate tests in the mask that was actually implemented, is_snp and numalt == 1, and the first of them does most of the damage: of the five canonical codons named in Section 5.7.1, four fail is_snp outright and only pfdhps K540E is lost to the biallelic test. Describing the loss as the work of "the biallelic restriction" alone understates it. The restriction as a whole was presented, accepted and implemented as a routine quality filter, and Section 5.7.1 sets out what it removed.

5.2 Adding t-SNE and PCoA alongside UMAP

Problem and decision. The thesis workflow specifies UMAP. The user asked for alternative ordinations so that the three could be compared. Both were added to Stage 3, reading the same distance matrix, with a quantitative comparison table.

[Observed] Why it mattered more than expected. UMAP and t-SNE are non-linear and their inter-cluster distances carry no quantitative meaning, a caveat the original script already printed. PCoA is linear, so its axes are interpretable and carry explicit variance proportions. The PCoA result places 1.3 per cent of variation on the first two axes, and in the comparison table the Spearman correlation between embedded and original pairwise distances is 0.32 for PCoA, 0.12 for t-SNE and 0.03 for UMAP.

[Interpreted] With structure as weak as it is here, those numbers make PCoA considerably more informative than any visual impression of the UMAP plot, and they would not have been available from the specified workflow alone. A correlation of 0.03 means the UMAP layout preserves almost none of the metric structure it is drawn from — which the plot itself does not disclose.

[Interpreted] Stage 3 was not affected by the corrected re-run, nor by either rebuild of the marker panel, which touches no genome-wide stage. This is a conclusion drawn from code reading plus file timestamps rather than a direct observation: step03_umap.py loads config.DIST_MATRIX, which config.py defines as MalariaGEN's published genome-wide pairwise distance matrix rather than the MAF-filtered analysis matrix, so the change from 3,359 to 3,916 SNPs described in Section 5.7 does not touch it; and results/pcoa_eigenvalues.csv and results/dimred_comparison.csv both still carry the modification time of the Stage 3 run.

5.3 Ploidy: how to count alleles in a haploid parasite coded as diploid

Problem. Blood-stage P. falciparum is haploid, but Pf8 records genotypes in diploid form: 0/0 or 1/1 for a clonal call, 0/1 where reads support both alleles (a mixed infection). The supplied step04b_fst.py called scikit-allel's count_alleles on the diploid array, which counts two gene copies per isolate. The same cached counts feed TreeMix and BayPass.

Why this is a defect, not a convention. Hudson's FST carries a sample-size correction; TreeMix derives drift standard errors from counts; BayPass's XtX is standardised by them. Doubling every sample size therefore makes all three anticonservative — narrower intervals, more apparently significant edges, more apparent outliers.

[Observed] The earlier version of this section added "without changing the point estimates much". That is wrong, and wrong in kind. In Hudson's estimator the sample-size correction enters the numerator subtractively, as −p(1−p)/(n−1) per population, so doubling n inflates the point estimate, and the inflation is largest where n is smallest. Recomputed during the audit on the then-current matrix, the six pairwise values are 1.6 to 31 times larger under diploid counting than under haploid counting, and the maximum pairwise FST would have been 0.0127 for Coastal–Transitional rather than the ~0.002 actually reported. The correction is load-bearing for the headline number, and the earlier text undersold it.

[Calculated] One clause in that earlier text is withdrawn rather than kept. It said the three smallest-n comparisons would have appeared to be the most differentiated pairs "rather than the least", and the second half does not hold on either matrix. On the final haploid matrix the three Transitional-zone pairs (n = 25) are 0.00158, 0.00158 and 0.00047 against a six-pair range of 0.00045 to 0.00232 — ranks two, three and five of six. On the superseded matrix the audit measured them at 0.001245, 0.001219 and 0.000346, ranks three, four and six. Only Guinea–Transitional is among the least differentiated either way. What survives is the narrower and sufficient point: diploid counting would have lifted the smallest-n pair to the top of the matrix from third place.

Options put to the user.

Haploid, mixed calls to missing — use the pipeline's existing --het-to-missing switch, then count one allele per isolate. Simplest; discards the mixed calls entirely.

Haploid, mixed calls to the majority allele (chosen) — assign the allele with more reads, ties to missing, then count one allele per isolate. Retains the information in the read depths.

Leave as written — point estimates move substantially upward and uncertainty stays understated throughout.

Implementation and verification. A function resolve_mixed_calls was added to step04a_prepare_callset.py, reading AD a block at a time only for SNPs where some retained isolate carries a mixed call. A companion isolate_allele_counts was added to callset_io.py; it takes the first gene copy once mixed calls are resolved, and falls back to diploid counting with an explicit warning if any mixed call remains. Both were verified against a hand-computed synthetic case (3 SNPs × 4 isolates, including a tie and a missing genotype) before being applied to real data.

[Observed] On the final dataset, 194,167 mixed calls were resolved: 129,665 to reference, 55,582 to alternate, and 8,920 tied and set to missing. The earlier version of this section reported a "residual heterozygous call rate" of 0.0000 to four decimal places; that figure is real but it is not residual.
